Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, honestly, the main thing is that I can’t seem to stay seated when I’m supposed to. It sounds kind of small when I say it like that, but it’s becoming noticeable in situations where I really need to be still and listen.

Like, during client interviews for work, I’ll tell myself, “Just sit here, pay attention, don’t move around,” and then a few minutes later I’m up—getting water, looking at something across the room, checking a file I probably could’ve waited to check. And it’s not just work. I tutor sometimes, and even then I’ll keep popping up instead of staying at the table. Even at book club, where the whole point is to sit and talk, I’m up and down enough that people have commented or given me looks.

I guess what brings me in is that I’m starting to feel embarrassed because I can’t just sit and listen like everyone else seems to. It’s not that I don’t care or that I’m trying to be rude. I just feel like I have to get up, and people are starting to notice.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, no, this definitely isn’t new. I mean, it feels more embarrassing now because I’m supposed to be a grown professional and sit still in front of clients, but if I’m being honest, this goes way back.

My grade-school report cards pretty much all had some version of, “She’s bright, but she can’t stay in her seat,” or “has trouble waiting her turn.” My mother still jokes about it, actually—she says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through, which is funny now because of what I do for work, but apparently I was like that as a kid too. Not just fidgety, but up and moving when everyone else was seated.

So, yeah… lifelong pattern, I’d say. It’s just become harder to hide or laugh off.

========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n2\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F41.1, F40.1, E05.9, F30.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, welcome in. I'm Dr. Smith. To start, can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, the main thing is that I can’t seem to stay seated when I’m supposed to. It sounds kind of small when I say it like that, but it’s becoming noticeable in situations where I really need to be still and listen.\n\nLike, during client interviews for work, I’ll tell myself, “Just sit here, pay attention, don’t move around,” and then a few minutes later I’m up—getting water, looking at something across the room, checking a file I probably could’ve waited to check. And it’s not just work. I tutor sometimes, and even then I’ll keep popping up instead of staying at the table. Even at book club, where the whole point is to sit and talk, I’m up and down enough that people have commented or given me looks.\n\nI guess what brings me in is that I’m starting to feel embarrassed because I can’t just sit and listen like everyone else seems to. It’s not that I don’t care or that I’m trying to be rude. I just feel like I have to get up, and people are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if this kind of restlessness and difficulty staying seated has been a lifelong pattern for you, even back in childhood, or is this something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, this definitely isn’t new. I mean, it feels more embarrassing now because I’m supposed to be a grown professional and sit still in front of clients, but if I’m being honest, this goes way back.\n\nMy grade-school report cards pretty much all had some version of, “She’s bright, but she can’t stay in her seat,” or “has trouble waiting her turn.” My mother still jokes about it, actually—she says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through, which is funny now because of what I do for work, but apparently I was like that as a kid too. Not just fidgety, but up and moving when everyone else was seated.\n\nSo, yeah… lifelong pattern, I’d say. It’s just become harder to hide or laugh off.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s not really tied to a worry, no. I mean, sometimes I’ll feel embarrassed after I realize I’ve gotten up again, especially if someone notices, but the urge itself doesn’t start with me thinking, “Oh no, what if something bad happens,” or anything like that.

It’s more like… I’m sitting there, and I know I’m supposed to stay put—like in a client interview, or when I’m tutoring, or even at book club—and then my body just sort of decides it needs to move. I’ll suddenly be up getting water or checking something or shifting around the room, and only afterward I’m like, “Why couldn’t I just sit and listen like everyone else?” So it happens even when I’m not especially worried. It’s more of this restless need to get up than an anxious thought.

willing to debrief with me because they feel steamrolled.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 8 months\n  manifestation: This has been noticeably worse for about 8 months, since I had to move out of my old Chicago apartment.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / private_detective_or_investigator / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S011\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, welcome in. I'm Dr. Smith. To start, can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, the main thing is that I can’t seem to stay seated when I’m supposed to. It sounds kind of small when I say it like that, but it’s becoming noticeable in situations where I really need to be still and listen.\n\nLike, during client interviews for work, I’ll tell myself, “Just sit here, pay attention, don’t move around,” and then a few minutes later I’m up—getting water, looking at something across the room, checking a file I probably could’ve waited to check. And it’s not just work. I tutor sometimes, and even then I’ll keep popping up instead of staying at the table. Even at book club, where the whole point is to sit and talk, I’m up and down enough that people have commented or given me looks.\n\nI guess what brings me in is that I’m starting to feel embarrassed because I can’t just sit and listen like everyone else seems to. It’s not that I don’t care or that I’m trying to be rude. I just feel like I have to get up, and people are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if this kind of restlessness and difficulty staying seated has been a lifelong pattern for you, even back in childhood, or is this something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, this definitely isn’t new. I mean, it feels more embarrassing now because I’m supposed to be a grown professional and sit still in front of clients, but if I’m being honest, this goes way back.\n\nMy grade-school report cards pretty much all had some version of, “She’s bright, but she can’t stay in her seat,” or “has trouble waiting her turn.” My mother still jokes about it, actually—she says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through, which is funny now because of what I do for work, but apparently I was like that as a kid too. Not just fidgety, but up and moving when everyone else was seated.\n\nSo, yeah… lifelong pattern, I’d say. It’s just become harder to hide or laugh off.\n\nDoctor: When you feel the urge to get up and move, is it usually linked to a specific worry or anxious thought running through your mind, or does it happen even when you're not feeling worried about anything in particular?\n\nPatient: It’s not really tied to a worry, no. I mean, sometimes I’ll feel embarrassed after I realize I’ve gotten up again, especially if someone notices, but the urge itself doesn’t start with me thinking, “Oh no, what if something bad happens,” or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like… I’m sitting there, and I know I’m supposed to stay put—like in a client interview, or when I’m tutoring, or even at book club—and then my body just sort of decides it needs to move. I’ll suddenly be up getting water or checking something or shifting around the room, and only afterward I’m like, “Why couldn’t I just sit and listen like everyone else?” So it happens even when I’m not especially worried. It’s more of this restless need to get up than an anxious thought.\n\n[Last Question]\nAside from the restlessness, do you also notice that your mind wanders or you have trouble keeping your attention on tasks, conversations, or details—like losing focus partway through something or being easily distracted?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, everyone’s mind wanders sometimes, right? So I don’t want to overstate it or make it sound like I’m zoning out constantly.

The thing I notice most is really the physical part—the needing to get up, move around, do something with my body. As far as my attention, I can usually follow what someone is saying if I’m engaged, especially with work stuff. I might shift around or stand up while I’m listening, but I don’t know that I’m losing the thread in a major way.

Details… I guess I’d have to think about that. I’m not coming in because I’m missing details or forgetting conversations, at least not that I’ve really noticed. It’s more that I can be sitting there trying to pay attention and my body is practically shouting at me to move. So if you’re asking whether my mind is wandering the same way my body is restless, I don’t know that I’d say that. Maybe sometimes, but nothing that stands out to me the way the restlessness does.

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Can you tell me if this kind of restlessness and difficulty staying seated has been a lifelong pattern for you, even back in childhood, or is this something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, this definitely isn’t new. I mean, it feels more embarrassing now because I’m supposed to be a grown professional and sit still in front of clients, but if I’m being honest, this goes way back.\n\nMy grade-school report cards pretty much all had some version of, “She’s bright, but she can’t stay in her seat,” or “has trouble waiting her turn.” My mother still jokes about it, actually—she says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through, which is funny now because of what I do for work, but apparently I was like that as a kid too. Not just fidgety, but up and moving when everyone else was seated.\n\nSo, yeah… lifelong pattern, I’d say. It’s just become harder to hide or laugh off.\n\nDoctor: When you feel the urge to get up and move, is it usually linked to a specific worry or anxious thought running through your mind, or does it happen even when you're not feeling worried about anything in particular?\n\nPatient: It’s not really tied to a worry, no. I mean, sometimes I’ll feel embarrassed after I realize I’ve gotten up again, especially if someone notices, but the urge itself doesn’t start with me thinking, “Oh no, what if something bad happens,” or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like… I’m sitting there, and I know I’m supposed to stay put—like in a client interview, or when I’m tutoring, or even at book club—and then my body just sort of decides it needs to move. I’ll suddenly be up getting water or checking something or shifting around the room, and only afterward I’m like, “Why couldn’t I just sit and listen like everyone else?” So it happens even when I’m not especially worried. So I don’t want to overstate it or make it sound like I’m zoning out constantly.\n\nThe thing I notice most is really the physical part—the needing to get up, move around, do something with my body. As far as my attention, I can usually follow what someone is saying if I’m engaged, especially with work stuff. I might shift around or stand up while I’m listening, but I don’t know that I’m losing the thread in a major way.\n\nDetails… I guess I’d have to think about that. I’m not coming in because I’m missing details or forgetting conversations, at least not that I’ve really noticed. It’s more that I can be sitting there trying to pay attention and my body is practically shouting at me to move. So if you’re asking whether my mind is wandering the same way my body is restless, I don’t know that I’d say that. Maybe sometimes, but nothing that stands out to me the way the restlessness does.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often find yourself interrupting people, blurting out answers or comments before someone finishes speaking, or having trouble waiting your turn in conversations or lines?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / private_detective_or_investigator / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Interrupting_Intruding\nmanifestation: \"If I hear junior detectives discussing a lead or a neighbor talking in the hallway, I jump in with questions or advice before I've been asked, and afterward I realize I barged into someone else's conversation.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I do. More than I like to admit, actually.

It’s not always in a dramatic way, like I’m cutting someone off mid-sentence on purpose, but I’ll hear something and my mouth is already moving before I’ve really checked whether I was invited into the conversation. At work, if a couple of the junior detectives are talking through a lead or debating what to do next, I’ll just jump in with, “Did you ask this?” or “No, look at the timing here,” or I’ll start giving advice before anyone has asked me to weigh in. And sometimes I can tell from their faces that I’ve kind of bulldozed my way into it.

It happens outside work too. Like if a neighbor is talking in the hallway and I overhear some detail, I’ll pop in with a question or suggestion, and then two seconds later I’m thinking, “Why did I just insert myself into that?” It’s embarrassing because in my head I’m being helpful, or I’m excited because I see a connection, but afterward I realize I barged into someone else’s conversation.

Waiting my turn conversationally is definitely hard when I feel like I know where something is going or I have a question that feels urgent. I’m trying to be better about pausing, but yes, that’s a real thing for me.

At work, if a couple of the junior detectives are talking through a lead or debating what to do next, I’ll just jump in with, “Did you ask this?” or “No, look at the timing here,” or I’ll start giving advice before anyone has asked me to weigh in. And sometimes I can tell from their faces that I’ve kind of bulldozed my way into it.\n\nIt happens outside work too. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s been pretty consistent lately, I’d say—not like a clear “episode” that starts and stops. The noticeable change has been over the last… about eight months, I think. Since I had to move out of my old apartment in Chicago.

Before that, I mean, I was always a little on the go, I guess, but this has felt more obvious and harder to ignore since then. It’s not like I have a few days where I’m suddenly a totally different person and then it disappears for weeks. It’s more day-to-day, this ongoing restlessness and blurting or jumping in.

The timing is what stands out to me: moving out of that apartment was a big disruption, and since then it’s just been worse in a way I can actually point to. So if you’re asking whether it comes in distinct stretches, I don’t think so. It’s more like it became noticeably worse around eight months ago and has kind of stayed that way.

It’s more that I can be sitting there trying to pay attention and my body is practically shouting at me to move. So if you’re asking whether my mind is wandering the same way my body is restless, I don’t know that I’d say that. Maybe sometimes, but nothing that stands out to me the way the restlessness does.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting people, blurting out answers or comments before someone finishes speaking, or having trouble waiting your turn in conversations or lines?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. More than I like to admit, actually.\n\nIt’s not always in a dramatic way, like I’m cutting someone off mid-sentence on purpose, but I’ll hear something and my mouth is already moving before I’ve really checked whether I was invited into the conversation. At work, if a couple of the junior detectives are talking through a lead or debating what to do next, I’ll just jump in with, “Did you ask this?” or “No, look at the timing here,” or I’ll start giving advice before anyone has asked me to weigh in. And sometimes I can tell from their faces that I’ve kind of bulldozed my way into it.\n\nIt happens outside work too. Like if a neighbor is talking in the hallway and I overhear some detail, I’ll pop in with a question or suggestion, and then two seconds later I’m thinking, “Why did I just insert myself into that?” It’s embarrassing because in my head I’m being helpful, or I’m excited because I see a connection, but afterward I realize I barged into someone else’s conversation.\n\nWaiting my turn conversationally is definitely hard when I feel like I know where something is going or I have a question that feels urgent. I’m trying to be better about pausing, but yes, that’s a real thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Does this restlessness and impulsivity happen fairly consistently, day in and day out, or does it come and go in distinct episodes lasting days to weeks where you also notice things like decreased need for sleep, racing thoughts, or an unusually elevated mood?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty consistent lately, I’d say—not like a clear “episode” that starts and stops. The noticeable change has been over the last… about eight months, I think. Since I had to move out of my old apartment in Chicago.\n\nBefore that, I mean, I was always a little on the go, I guess, but this has felt more obvious and harder to ignore since then. It’s not like I have a few days where I’m suddenly a totally different person and then it disappears for weeks. It’s more day-to-day, this ongoing restlessness and blurting or jumping in.\n\nThe timing is what stands out to me: moving out of that apartment was a big disruption, and since then it’s just been worse in a way I can actually point to. So if you’re asking whether it comes in distinct stretches, I don’t think so. It’s more like it became noticeably worse around eight months ago and has kind of stayed that way.\n\n[Last Question]\nThinking back to your school years, did teachers or your mother ever mention you seeming distracted, losing focus on tasks, forgetting things, or making careless mistakes—separate from the restlessness?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I honestly don’t remember that being a big thing, no. I’m trying to think back carefully, because school was a long time ago and I don’t want to give you a clean answer if my memory is fuzzy.

My mother definitely got comments about me being… energetic, talkative, hard to keep seated, that sort of thing. But distracted in the sense of not following the lesson, losing track of assignments, careless mistakes, forgetting things all the time—I don’t have a strong memory of that being what adults focused on. If it happened, it wasn’t something that stuck as a repeated complaint.

I did okay academically overall, from what I remember. I mean, I wasn’t some perfectly organized little student, but I don’t recall teachers saying, “She can’t pay attention,” or “She’s always missing details.” It was more that I had too much to say or too much energy. So separate from the restlessness, I’m not sure I can really point to much.

It’s not that I don’t care or that I’m trying to be rude. I’m not sure I can give you anything solid there. I haven’t really tracked it in a careful way, if I’m honest.

Caffeine-wise, I mean… I drink coffee, but I don’t have a neat before-and-after number in my head where I can say, “Yes, eight months ago I doubled it,” or anything like that. It’s more one of those background habits I don’t always pay attention to unless someone specifically asks me. I haven’t noticed some obvious new pattern that lines up cleanly with all of this.

So the honest answer is: I haven’t noticed a clear change in caffeine, substances, or a new medical problem that explains it, but I also haven’t been keeping a spreadsheet on myself.

It’s not that I don’t care or that I’m trying to be rude. I just feel like I have to get up, and people are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if this kind of restlessness and difficulty staying seated has been a lifelong pattern for you, even back in childhood, or is this something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, this definitely isn’t new. I mean, it feels more embarrassing now because I’m supposed to be a grown professional and sit still in front of clients, but if I’m being honest, this goes way back.\n\nMy grade-school report cards pretty much all had some version of, “She’s bright, but she can’t stay in her seat,” or “has trouble waiting her turn.” My mother still jokes about it, actually—she says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through, which is funny now because of what I do for work, but apparently I was like that as a kid too. Not just fidgety, but up and moving when everyone else was seated.\n\nSo, yeah… lifelong pattern, I’d say. It’s just become harder to hide or laugh off.\n\nDoctor: When you feel the urge to get up and move, is it usually linked to a specific worry or anxious thought running through your mind, or does it happen even when you're not feeling worried about anything in particular?\n\nPatient: It’s not really tied to a worry, no. I mean, sometimes I’ll feel embarrassed after I realize I’ve gotten up again, especially if someone notices, but the urge itself doesn’t start with me thinking, “Oh no, what if something bad happens,” or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like… I’m sitting there, and I know I’m supposed to stay put—like in a client interview, or when I’m tutoring, or even at book club—and then my body just sort of decides it needs to move. I’ll suddenly be up getting water or checking something or shifting around the room, and only afterward I’m like, “Why couldn’t I just sit and listen like everyone else?” So it happens even when I’m not especially worried. It’s more of this restless need to get up than an anxious thought.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the restlessness, do you also notice that your mind wanders or you have trouble keeping your attention on tasks, conversations, or details—like losing focus partway through something or being easily distracted?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, everyone’s mind wanders sometimes, right? So I don’t want to overstate it or make it sound like I’m zoning out constantly.\n\nThe thing I notice most is really the physical part—the needing to get up, move around, do something with my body. As far as my attention, I can usually follow what someone is saying if I’m engaged, especially with work stuff. I might shift around or stand up while I’m listening, but I don’t know that I’m losing the thread in a major way.\n\nDetails… I guess I’d have to think about that. I’m not coming in because I’m missing details or forgetting conversations, at least not that I’ve really noticed. It’s more that I can be sitting there trying to pay attention and my body is practically shouting at me to move. So if you’re asking whether my mind is wandering the same way my body is restless, I don’t know that I’d say that. Maybe sometimes, but nothing that stands out to me the way the restlessness does.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting people, blurting out answers or comments before someone finishes speaking, or having trouble waiting your turn in conversations or lines?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. More than I like to admit, actually.\n\nIt’s not always in a dramatic way, like I’m cutting someone off mid-sentence on purpose, but I’ll hear something and my mouth is already moving before I’ve really checked whether I was invited into the conversation. At work, if a couple of the junior detectives are talking through a lead or debating what to do next, I’ll just jump in with, “Did you ask this?” or “No, look at the timing here,” or I’ll start giving advice before anyone has asked me to weigh in. And sometimes I can tell from their faces that I’ve kind of bulldozed my way into it.\n\nIt happens outside work too. Like if a neighbor is talking in the hallway and I overhear some detail, I’ll pop in with a question or suggestion, and then two seconds later I’m thinking, “Why did I just insert myself into that?” It’s embarrassing because in my head I’m being helpful, or I’m excited because I see a connection, but afterward I realize I barged into someone else’s conversation.\n\nWaiting my turn conversationally is definitely hard when I feel like I know where something is going or I have a question that feels urgent. I’m trying to be better about pausing, but yes, that’s a real thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Does this restlessness and impulsivity happen fairly consistently, day in and day out, or does it come and go in distinct episodes lasting days to weeks where you also notice things like decreased need for sleep, racing thoughts, or an unusually elevated mood?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty consistent lately, I’d say—not like a clear “episode” that starts and stops. The noticeable change has been over the last… about eight months, I think. Since I had to move out of my old apartment in Chicago.\n\nBefore that, I mean, I was always a little on the go, I guess, but this has felt more obvious and harder to ignore since then. It’s not like I have a few days where I’m suddenly a totally different person and then it disappears for weeks. It’s more day-to-day, this ongoing restlessness and blurting or jumping in.\n\nThe timing is what stands out to me: moving out of that apartment was a big disruption, and since then it’s just been worse in a way I can actually point to. So if you’re asking whether it comes in distinct stretches, I don’t think so. It’s more like it became noticeably worse around eight months ago and has kind of stayed that way.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back to your school years, did teachers or your mother ever mention you seeming distracted, losing focus on tasks, forgetting things, or making careless mistakes—separate from the restlessness?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t remember that being a big thing, no. I’m trying to think back carefully, because school was a long time ago and I don’t want to give you a clean answer if my memory is fuzzy.\n\nMy mother definitely got comments about me being… energetic, talkative, hard to keep seated, that sort of thing. But distracted in the sense of not following the lesson, losing track of assignments, careless mistakes, forgetting things all the time—I don’t have a strong memory of that being what adults focused on. If it happened, it wasn’t something that stuck as a repeated complaint.\n\nI did okay academically overall, from what I remember. I mean, I wasn’t some perfectly organized little student, but I don’t recall teachers saying, “She can’t pay attention,” or “She’s always missing details.” It was more that I had too much to say or too much energy. So separate from the restlessness, I’m not sure I can really point to much.\n\nDoctor: Over these past eight months, has there been any change in your caffeine intake, use of stimulant medications or other substances, or any new medical issues like thyroid problems that could be contributing to how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you anything solid there. I haven’t really tracked it in a careful way, if I’m honest.\n\nCaffeine-wise, I mean… I drink coffee, but I don’t have a neat before-and-after number in my head where I can say, “Yes, eight months ago I doubled it,” or anything like that. It’s more one of those background habits I don’t always pay attention to unless someone specifically asks me. I haven’t noticed some obvious new pattern that lines up cleanly with all of this.\n\nAs far as stimulant medications or other substances… I don’t think there’s a clear change I can point to. Nothing is jumping out at me as, “Oh, that started and then all this started.” And medically, I don’t know of any thyroid issue or new condition that someone has told me about. I’m not saying it’s impossible—I’m 49, so I know things can sneak up on you—but I don’t have anything specific there.\n\nSo the honest answer is: I haven’t noticed a clear change in caffeine, substances, or a new medical problem that explains it, but I also haven’t been keeping a spreadsheet on myself."

Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.     "S012": "The patient clearly describes physical restlessness, including needing to get up, move around, and feeling as though their body is 'shouting' at them to move.",
    "S001": "The patient states they are not coming in because they are missing details and have not really noticed this as a problem.",
    "S002": "The patient says they do not feel they are zoning out constantly and does not describe sustained attention problems as standing out.",
    "S003": "The patient reports they can usually follow what someone is saying when engaged and does not feel they are losing the thread in a major way."
  }

Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nYes… I do. More than I like to admit, actually.\n\nIt’s not always in a dramatic way, like I’m cutting someone off mid-sentence on purpose, but I’ll hear something and my mouth is already moving before I’ve really checked whether I was invited into the conversation. At work, if a couple of the junior detectives are talking through a lead or debating what to do next, I’ll just jump in with, “Did you ask this?” or “No, look at the timing here,” or I’ll start giving advice before anyone has asked me to weigh in. And sometimes I can tell from their faces that I’ve kind of bulldozed my way into it.\n\nIt happens outside work too. Like if a neighbor is talking in the hallway and I overhear some detail, I’ll pop in with a question or suggestion, and then two seconds later I’m thinking, “Why did I just insert myself into that?” It’s embarrassing because in my head I’m being helpful, or I’m excited because I see a connection, but afterward I realize I barged into someone else’s conversation.\n\nWaiting my turn conversationally is definitely hard when I feel like I know where something is going or I have a question that feels urgent. I’m trying to be better about pausing, but yes, that’s a real thing for me.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI honestly don’t remember that being a big thing, no. I’m trying to think back carefully, because school was a long time ago and I don’t want to give you a clean answer if my memory is fuzzy.\n\nMy mother definitely got comments about me being… energetic, talkative, hard to keep seated, that sort of thing. But distracted in the sense of not following the lesson, losing track of assignments, careless mistakes, forgetting things all the time—I don’t have a strong memory of that being what adults focused on. If it happened, it wasn’t something that stuck as a repeated complaint.\n\nI did okay academically overall, from what I remember. I mean, I wasn’t some perfectly organized little student, but I don’t recall teachers saying, “She can’t pay attention,” or “She’s always missing details.” It was more that I had too much to say or too much energy. So separate from the restlessness, I’m not sure I can really point to much.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
